========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Any area where I don't worry? I... I wish I could give you a different answer, doctor, I really do. But honestly, no. There isn't a single quiet corner left in my head. It feels like my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things, constantly, without any sort of break. 

I'll be trying to eat breakfast, and suddenly I'm obsessing over whether my old patients are being looked after properly. I mean, I've treated some of these families for three generations. I know their dental histories, I know how nervous Mrs. Gable gets before a root canal, I know exactly how to adjust the chair for Mr. Henderson's bad back. Is the new associate taking the time to do all that, or is he just rushing them in and out like an assembly line? 

And then, before I can even convince myself they're fine, my brain jumps right over to the business side of things. I start agonizing over whether the practice handoff is financially sound. I've looked at the spreadsheets a hundred times, the brokers assure me the valuation is solid, the lawyers have reviewed it... but what if there's a loophole? What if the market dips, or the buyout structure falls apart, and everything I've built for the last forty years just crumbles financially at the eleventh hour? 

And it’s not just the clinic, either. It’s me. It’s this deep, creeping dread about what happens after the papers are actually signed. I keep wondering whether I’ll just become completely useless once I’m fully retired. I’ve been a dentist my entire adult life. People needed me to fix their pain. If I'm not doing that, if I don't have a schedule and a staff and patients relying on my expertise, who am I? Just some old man puttering around a garden that he can't even find the energy to weed right now? 

It just bleeds into absolutely every corner of my life. And the most frustrating part—the part that really makes me feel like I'm losing my grip—is that I know, logically, that this worry feels out of proportion, even to me. I know the contracts are standard. I know the new associate graduated top of his class from a fantastic program. I know I have enough savings to retire comfortably. But my brain just refuses to accept the facts. It just keeps spinning and spinning, finding new things to latch onto, and I just can't seem to find a single area of my life that it hasn't infected.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's... it's a bit of a culmination of things, I suppose, but what finally pushed me to make this appointment—and my wife has been nudging me about it for a while now, to be perfectly honest—is that my concentration has just become entirely unreliable. It's really starting to affect my day-to-day. For instance, I'm a dentist, have been for nearly forty years, and lately, I'll be sitting in the back office reviewing a patient's chart before going into the operatory. I'll read a line about their periodontal history, and then I'll realize I've just read that exact same line three times. My eyes are moving over the words, but my mind is a million miles away, just spinning. \n\nAnd it's not just at the clinic, either. In my downtime, I like to paint—watercolors, mostly landscapes, it usually helps me unwind. But just last weekend, I was trying to lay out a simple blue wash for a sky, a technique I've done a thousand times, and I completely lost my focus. My brush just hovered there because my mind had wandered off, completely hijacked by this cascading list of everything that could possibly go wrong. I start thinking about the clinic's lease renewal, or if my grandson is doing okay in his new school, or if that slight rattle in the car engine means the transmission is failing... and suddenly I'm not painting, I'm not reading the chart, I'm just sitting there paralyzed by all these thoughts. It's exhausting, frankly, just trying to keep my brain on the task right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes, doctor, that's... that's exactly it. It's almost constant. I mean, it's like a motor running in the background that I just can't switch off, no matter how hard I try. I'll wake up, and before my feet even hit the floor, my mind is already off to the races. I'm 64 now, you see, and I'm in the process of gradually stepping back from the practice—bringing in a younger associate to take over. And logically, I know it's the right time. But my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things, constantly. I worry about whether my old patients—people I've seen for decades, whose kids I've treated—are being looked after properly by the new guy. I mean, his technique is fine, but does he have the bedside manner? \n\nAnd then it spirals from there. I start agonizing over whether the practice handoff is financially sound, if we structured the buyout correctly, or if the market is going to tank and leave me without the retirement cushion I planned on. And then, and this is the one that really gets me, I start worrying about what happens to me. Will I become completely useless once I’m fully retired? I've been a dentist my whole adult life. If I'm not doing that, who am I? It just goes on and on like that. And the frustrating part is, I know it's irrational. The worry feels entirely out of proportion, even to me. I can look at the financials and see they're fine, but the feeling of impending doom just doesn't care about the spreadsheets. It's just... always there, humming away, making me second-guess absolutely everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes and includes symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I've ever had anything quite like that. It's not... it's not a sudden explosion, if you know what I mean. It's more of a slow burn, this constant, simmering tension that just never seems to boil over but also never cools down. I don't get those moments where my heart is pounding out of my chest or I'm gasping for air, like you see in the movies when someone is having a panic attack. My wife's sister, actually, she gets those. She had one at our Thanksgiving dinner a few years ago—just completely froze up, couldn't breathe, had to go lay down in the guest bedroom for an hour. It was terrifying for her, I'm sure. \n\nBut for me, it's not like that. My heart rate might tick up a bit when I'm really dwelling on the practice buyout, or if I'm lying in bed at 3 a.m. running through the worst-case scenarios for my retirement portfolio, but it's not that sudden, overwhelming wave of terror you're describing. It's just... the worry is always there, kind of sitting on my shoulder, whispering in my ear. It's exhausting, but it doesn't peak and then go away. It just hums along, day in and day out, like a refrigerator motor you can't ignore. So, no, I haven't had those intense, out-of-the-blue episodes of panic or shortness of breath. Just the endless, grinding worry.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry spread across many different aspects of your life or is it mainly focused on one or two specific areas, like your retirement or the dental practice?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's... it's definitely not just one or two things, as much as I wish it were that simple. It really does spread out like a spiderweb across just about everything. I mean, yes, the retirement and the practice are the big, obvious ones right now. I mentioned how my mind keeps circling around all sorts of things with that—like, whether my old patients, people I've known for thirty years, are being looked after properly by the new associate. And then I worry about whether the practice handoff is financially sound, if we dotted every 'i' and crossed every 't' on the contracts. And, of course, the big existential dread of whether I’ll become completely useless once I’m fully retired and not going into the clinic every day. \n\nBut it doesn't stop there. It bleeds into everything else. I'll worry about my wife's health, even though she just had a physical and she's perfectly fine. I'll worry about my adult children—my daughter lives in Chicago, and if she doesn't text me back within a few hours, my brain immediately jumps to, 'Did she get into a car accident? Is she okay?' And then there's the house. We have an older home, built in the 70s, and every little creak in the floorboards makes me think the foundation is settling and we're going to need fifty thousand dollars in repairs. Or I'll watch the evening news and suddenly I'm agonizing over the state of the economy, or global politics, things I have absolutely zero control over. \n\nIt's just... it's exhausting because it latches onto whatever is in front of me. If I fix one problem, my brain just finds another one to chew on. And the worst part is, the worry feels completely out of proportion even to me. I know it's irrational to think my daughter is in a ditch just because she's in a meeting and can't check her phone, or to think the house is collapsing because of a squeaky floorboard. I know this logically. But the feeling, that heavy, churning anxiety, it just doesn't listen to logic. It just keeps spinning and spinning, jumping from the practice to the family to the house to the news, on and on. It's like I'm constantly bracing for an impact that never actually comes, but I can't stop bracing for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Restless... on edge... you mean like physically? Like pacing around the room or not being able to sit still? No, I don't think I'd say that. Actually, it's funny you ask, because physically, I'm almost the exact opposite. \n\nBeing a dentist for, gosh, nearly forty years now, you really have to train your body to be incredibly still. When you're doing a complex root canal or trying to place a crown perfectly, you have to hold your hands and your posture absolutely steady for long, long stretches of time. You're hunched over, hyper-focused, barely moving a muscle except your fingers. So my body is very used to just staying put, even when things are tense. \n\nEven at home, when my mind is racing a mile a minute about the practice buyout or whether my daughter in Chicago is safe, I'm usually just sitting completely still in my recliner. My wife will tell you—I can sit in that old leather chair in the den for three hours straight, staring at the evening news without really watching a single story, just completely lost in my own head, running through all those worst-case scenarios I mentioned. I don't pace the floors, I don't bounce my knee up and down, I don't fidget with my hands like some people do when they're nervous. It's really all trapped up here, in my brain. The mental gears are just grinding away, constantly, but the rest of my body is just... sitting there, perfectly quiet. So, no, I haven't really noticed feeling physically on edge or restless, like I need to jump out of my skin or move around. It's just my thoughts that refuse to sit still.\n\nDoctor: Has this constant worrying been interfering with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it has been getting in the way, more than I’d like to admit. It’s... it's frustrating, really. You see, with the practice, I’ve actually had to slow down the whole retirement handoff process. I was supposed to be transitioning more of the complex cases to the new associate by now, but I just keep second-guessing every single decision. I’ll look at a treatment plan we discussed, and my mind will just start spiraling—what if he misses a hidden canal? What if the margins on the crown aren't perfect and the patient comes back in pain in six months? So I end up stepping in, re-checking everything, taking over cases I should be handing off. It’s causing me to lose so much time at the clinic, and I know it’s probably frustrating the new associate, too, though he’s too polite to say anything. \n\nAnd it’s not just work, either. Even my hobbies... things I used to do to unwind. I’ve painted watercolors for twenty years, mostly landscapes, and I used to love spending a Sunday afternoon out in the garden. But lately, even those things don't feel as manageable as they used to. I’ll set up my easel, and instead of focusing on the canvas, I’m just sitting there, brush in hand, worrying about the clinic contracts or my daughter in Chicago. Or I’ll be out in the garden, trying to prune the roses, and I’m just consumed by this dread about the economy or the house foundation. I can't just be present and enjoy it anymore. The worry just kind of crowds everything else out, making even the simple, enjoyable things feel like a chore because my brain is just so preoccupied with all these... these endless scenarios. So, yes, it’s definitely taking a toll on how I function day-to-day.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Distress... yes, absolutely. It's profoundly distressing, doctor. I mean, it's not just a passing thought, like when you remember you forgot to take the trash out. It's this heavy, suffocating blanket that just settles over me. \n\nYou know, I've always prided myself on being a very capable person. As a dentist, people come to you in pain, and you fix it. You take control, you use your expertise, and you solve the problem. I've spent forty years doing that. But with this... this constant churning in my head, I can't fix it. I can't drill it out or put a crown on it. And that feeling of helplessness, of being a prisoner to my own runaway thoughts, is just incredibly upsetting. \n\nI'll be sitting there in my recliner at night, trying to watch a documentary with my wife—something about history, usually, something that should be relaxing—and instead of listening to the narrator, my chest feels tight, my stomach is in knots, and I'm just agonizing over the wording in section four of the transition contract, or wondering if my daughter remembered to get her snow tires put on before the blizzard hit Chicago. And I know it's ruining my evening, I know it's ruining my peace of mind, but I just can't turn the valve off. \n\nIt makes me feel so defeated. I've worked my whole life to get to this point, to be able to retire comfortably, to enjoy my family and my painting and my garden. This is supposed to be the golden time, isn't it? The reward for all those years of hard work. But instead, I'm just miserable. I'm exhausted from fighting my own brain from the moment I wake up until I finally manage to fall asleep. So, yes, it causes me a great deal of distress. It's stealing the joy right out of my life, and I just don't know how to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble meeting your responsibilities at work or at home due to your worrying?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose that goes right back to what I was just saying a few minutes ago about the practice. My main responsibility right now—my actual, primary job description at this stage in my career—is to manage this transition smoothly. But like I mentioned, I’ve had to officially slow down and postpone major parts of the retirement handoff. \n\nWe had a very strict timeline drawn up by the practice brokers, you see. I was supposed to have fully handed over the vendor accounts and the staff payroll management by the end of last month. But because I keep second-guessing every single decision, and obsessively re-checking the new associate's work like I told you, I'm losing enormous amounts of time at the clinic. I'll spend forty-five minutes agonizing over a file that should take me five minutes to approve. So I'm falling behind on the actual administrative responsibilities of the transition. The lawyers will send over the handoff documents, and instead of just reviewing and signing them, I stare at them for hours, paralyzed by all the \"what ifs.\" I'm just not meeting my obligations to the practice, to the new associate, or even to the brokers, because I'm just so bogged down in my own head. \n\nAnd at home... well, it’s the same story. I know I talked about my painting and my gardening earlier, and while you might just call those hobbies, keeping up the house and the yard is still a responsibility, you know? My wife relies on me for that. We have a large property, and it takes work. But lately, even those things just don't feel as manageable as they used to. The yard work is really piling up. The rose bushes need to be winterized, the raised beds need to be cleared out before the first hard freeze, and usually, I’d be right on top of it, out there every weekend until it was done perfectly. \n\nBut now? Now I just look out the window at the garden, and my brain is so exhausted from worrying about the clinic contracts and my daughter in Chicago and the state of the economy that I can't even bring myself to go out to the shed and get my tools. The canvases in my study are just sitting there blank, gathering dust, because I can't organize my thoughts enough to even mix the paints. So, yes, whether it's executing the clinic transition or just keeping up with my end of the bargain around the house, I'm definitely having trouble keeping up. The worry just eats up all my time and energy until there's absolutely nothing left for the things I'm actually supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Has your constant worrying affected your relationships with your family or friends?\n\nPatient: Relationships... well, you know, I’ve been thinking about that, and I wouldn't say it’s damaged anything, thank God. At least, not that I can see. \n\nMy wife, Eleanor, she’s an absolute saint. We’ll be married thirty-eight years this coming May, and she’s been with me through everything—dental school, taking out the loans to buy my first practice, raising the kids. So she knows me better than anyone. She knows I’ve always been a bit meticulous, a bit of a planner, though obviously this current situation is entirely different and much more severe. But she doesn't get angry with me. When I’m sitting there in the recliner, completely zoned out during one of our shows, she doesn't yell. She just sort of sighs, pauses the TV, and asks if I'm worrying about the transition contracts again. She’s incredibly patient. She'll make me a cup of chamomile tea and tell me to just put the paperwork out of my mind for the night. I know it must be exhausting for her to see me like this, and I feel terribly guilty about it, but we aren't fighting. I don't lash out at her, I just... I just retreat inward, I suppose. \n\nAnd as for my daughter in Chicago, Sarah... well, I probably call her a lot more often than a thirty-year-old woman wants to be called by her father. I’ll call her on a Tuesday afternoon just to make absolutely sure she remembered to get the oil changed in her Honda, or to remind her to double-lock her apartment door because I read some article about crime rates in her neighborhood. But she’s a good kid. She just laughs it off. She says, \"Yes, Dad, I did it, I'm fine.\" She doesn't get irritated, or if she does, she hides it well. \n\nEven with our friends... we have a regular dinner group, mostly a few other dentists from the local association and their wives. We went out just last Friday to this wonderful Italian place downtown, Luigi's, a place we've been going to for over a decade. And I have to admit, I wasn't exactly the life of the party. We were passing around the calamari, and I spent the entire appetizer course just staring at the tablecloth, agonizing over whether the new associate's malpractice insurance rider was filed correctly with the state board. But my friends, they just think I'm preoccupied with the logistics of retiring. They'll clap me on the shoulder and say, \"Hang in there, the golf course is calling!\" They don't realize the extent of the turmoil going on in my head. \n\nSo, no, I don't think it's driving people away or causing any real conflict. It's more of an internal torture, if that makes sense. I'm just incredibly lucky that the people around me are so understanding and give me so much grace, because I know I must be a bit of a drag to be around lately, constantly lost in my own head like this.\n\nDoctor: Is there any area of your life where you don't find yourself worrying?\n\nPatient: Any area where I don't worry? I... I wish I could give you a different answer, doctor, I really do. But honestly, no. There isn't a single quiet corner left in my head. It feels like my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things, constantly, without any sort of break. \n\nI'll be trying to eat breakfast, and suddenly I'm obsessing over whether my old patients are being looked after properly. I mean, I've treated some of these families for three generations. I know their dental histories, I know how nervous Mrs. Gable gets before a root canal, I know exactly how to adjust the chair for Mr. Henderson's bad back. Is the new associate taking the time to do all that, or is he just rushing them in and out like an assembly line? \n\nAnd then, before I can even convince myself they're fine, my brain jumps right over to the business side of things. I start agonizing over whether the practice handoff is financially sound. I've looked at the spreadsheets a hundred times, the brokers assure me the valuation is solid, the lawyers have reviewed it... but what if there's a loophole? What if the market dips, or the buyout structure falls apart, and everything I've built for the last forty years just crumbles financially at the eleventh hour? \n\nAnd it’s not just the clinic, either. It’s me. It’s this deep, creeping dread about what happens after the papers are actually signed. I keep wondering whether I’ll just become completely useless once I’m fully retired. I’ve been a dentist my entire adult life. People needed me to fix their pain. If I'm not doing that, if I don't have a schedule and a staff and patients relying on my expertise, who am I? Just some old man puttering around a garden that he can't even find the energy to weed right now? \n\nIt just bleeds into absolutely every corner of my life. And the most frustrating part—the part that really makes me feel like I'm losing my grip—is that I know, logically, that this worry feels out of proportion, even to me. I know the contracts are standard. I know the new associate graduated top of his class from a fantastic program. I know I have enough savings to retire comfortably. But my brain just refuses to accept the facts. It just keeps spinning and spinning, finding new things to latch onto, and I just can't seem to find a single area of my life that it hasn't infected.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

But she doesn't get angry with me. When I’m sitting there in the recliner, completely zoned out during one of our shows, she doesn't yell. She just sort of sighs, pauses the TV, and asks if I'm worrying about the transition contracts again. She’s incredibly patient. She'll make me a cup of chamomile tea and tell me to just put the paperwork out of my mind for the night. I know it must be exhausting for her to see me like this, and I feel terribly guilty about it, but we aren't fighting. I don't lash out at her, I just... I just retreat inward, I suppose. \n\nAnd as for my daughter in Chicago, Sarah... well, I probably call her a lot more often than a thirty-year-old woman wants to be called by her father. I’ll call her on a Tuesday afternoon just to make absolutely sure she remembered to get the oil changed in her Honda, or to remind her to double-lock her apartment door because I read some article about crime rates in her neighborhood. But she’s a good kid. She just laughs it off. She says, \"Yes, Dad, I did it, I'm fine.\" She doesn't get irritated, or if she does, she hides it well. \n\nEven with our friends... we have a regular dinner group, mostly a few other dentists from the local association and their wives. We went out just last Friday to this wonderful Italian place downtown, Luigi's, a place we've been going to for over a decade. And I have to admit, I wasn't exactly the life of the party. We were passing around the calamari, and I spent the entire appetizer course just staring at the tablecloth, agonizing over whether the new associate's malpractice insurance rider was filed correctly with the state board. But my friends, they just think I'm preoccupied with the logistics of retiring. They'll clap me on the shoulder and say, \"Hang in there, the golf course is calling!\" They don't realize the extent of the turmoil going on in my head. \n\nSo, no, I don't think it's driving people away or causing any real conflict. It's more of an internal torture, if that makes sense. I'm just incredibly lucky that the people around me are so understanding and give me so much grace, because I know I must be a bit of a drag to be around lately, constantly lost in my own head like this.\n\nDoctor: Is there any area of your life where you don't find yourself worrying?\n\nPatient: Any area where I don't worry? I... I wish I could give you a different answer, doctor, I really do. But honestly, no. There isn't a single quiet corner left in my head. It feels like my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things, constantly, without any sort of break. \n\nI'll be trying to eat breakfast, and suddenly I'm obsessing over whether my old patients are being looked after properly. I mean, I've treated some of these families for three generations. I know their dental histories, I know how nervous Mrs. Gable gets before a root canal, I know exactly how to adjust the chair for Mr. Henderson's bad back. Is the new associate taking the time to do all that, or is he just rushing them in and out like an assembly line? \n\nAnd then, before I can even convince myself they're fine, my brain jumps right over to the business side of things. I start agonizing over whether the practice handoff is financially sound. I've looked at the spreadsheets a hundred times, the brokers assure me the valuation is solid, the lawyers have reviewed it... but what if there's a loophole? What if the market dips, or the buyout structure falls apart, and everything I've built for the last forty years just crumbles financially at the eleventh hour? \n\nAnd it’s not just the clinic, either. It’s me. It’s this deep, creeping dread about what happens after the papers are actually signed. I keep wondering whether I’ll just become completely useless once I’m fully retired. I’ve been a dentist my entire adult life. People needed me to fix their pain. If I'm not doing that, if I don't have a schedule and a staff and patients relying on my expertise, who am I? Just some old man puttering around a garden that he can't even find the energy to weed right now? \n\nIt just bleeds into absolutely every corner of my life. And the most frustrating part—the part that really makes me feel like I'm losing my grip—is that I know, logically, that this worry feels out of proportion, even to me. I know the contracts are standard. I know the new associate graduated top of his class from a fantastic program. I know I have enough savings to retire comfortably. But my brain just refuses to accept the facts. It just keeps spinning and spinning, finding new things to latch onto, and I just can't seem to find a single area of my life that it hasn't infected.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Enjoy... well, I suppose "enjoy" is the operative word there, isn't it? Because I do still go to things. I haven't stopped showing up. Like I was just saying about that dinner at Luigi's with the other dentists, or... or even just sitting in the living room with Eleanor in the evenings. I don't find it difficult to actually be there, physically. I make a point of it, actually. 

We have a Sunday brunch group, too, at the local club—we've been going for, gosh, it must be fifteen, maybe sixteen years now. We always try to get the same table right by the window overlooking the ninth hole. And the guys will be going on and on about their golf swings, or pulling out their phones to show pictures of their grandkids, and I'm there. I'm physically present. I'm eating my eggs benedict and drinking my coffee and paying the check. So I am definitely still spending time with them. I haven't locked myself in the study or completely withdrawn from society or anything dramatic like that. 

But as for actually enjoying it? That’s... well, I'm not entirely sure. Because how can you truly enjoy a beautiful Sunday morning or a nice conversation when your brain is just buzzing, constantly buzzing, about transition timelines and lease agreements and whether the new associate is checking the margins on his crowns properly? I'm there, but I'm not really *there*, if that makes sense. I'm just sort of nodding and smiling, maybe throwing in a "that's great, Bob" here and there, while my mind is thirty miles away at the clinic, or worrying about Sarah's apartment security up in Chicago. 

So, I wouldn't say I find it difficult to spend the time with people—my routine hasn't really changed in that regard—but the actual joy of it, the relaxation you're supposed to get from being around your friends and your wife... I haven't really noticed much of that lately. I'm not sure I'm capable of it right now. The worry just sort of acts like a heavy blanket over the whole event, muffling all the good parts until we can just go home and I can get back to my spreadsheets.

It's really starting to affect my day-to-day. For instance, I'm a dentist, have been for nearly forty years, and lately, I'll be sitting in the back office reviewing a patient's chart before going into the operatory. I'll read a line about their periodontal history, and then I'll realize I've just read that exact same line three times. My eyes are moving over the words, but my mind is a million miles away, just spinning. \n\nAnd it's not just at the clinic, either. In my downtime, I like to paint—watercolors, mostly landscapes, it usually helps me unwind. But just last weekend, I was trying to lay out a simple blue wash for a sky, a technique I've done a thousand times, and I completely lost my focus. My brush just hovered there because my mind had wandered off, completely hijacked by this cascading list of everything that could possibly go wrong. I start thinking about the clinic's lease renewal, or if my grandson is doing okay in his new school, or if that slight rattle in the car engine means the transmission is failing... and suddenly I'm not painting, I'm not reading the chart, I'm just sitting there paralyzed by all these thoughts. It's exhausting, frankly, just trying to keep my brain on the task right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes, doctor, that's... that's exactly it. It's almost constant. I mean, it's like a motor running in the background that I just can't switch off, no matter how hard I try. I'll wake up, and before my feet even hit the floor, my mind is already off to the races. I'm 64 now, you see, and I'm in the process of gradually stepping back from the practice—bringing in a younger associate to take over. And logically, I know it's the right time. But my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things, constantly. I worry about whether my old patients—people I've seen for decades, whose kids I've treated—are being looked after properly by the new guy. I mean, his technique is fine, but does he have the bedside manner? \n\nAnd then it spirals from there. I start agonizing over whether the practice handoff is financially sound, if we structured the buyout correctly, or if the market is going to tank and leave me without the retirement cushion I planned on. And then, and this is the one that really gets me, I start worrying about what happens to me. Will I become completely useless once I’m fully retired? I've been a dentist my whole adult life. If I'm not doing that, who am I? It just goes on and on like that. And the frustrating part is, I know it's irrational. The worry feels entirely out of proportion, even to me. I can look at the financials and see they're fine, but the feeling of impending doom just doesn't care about the spreadsheets. It's just... always there, humming away, making me second-guess absolutely everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes and includes symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I've ever had anything quite like that. It's not... it's not a sudden explosion, if you know what I mean. It's more of a slow burn, this constant, simmering tension that just never seems to boil over but also never cools down. I don't get those moments where my heart is pounding out of my chest or I'm gasping for air, like you see in the movies when someone is having a panic attack. My wife's sister, actually, she gets those. She had one at our Thanksgiving dinner a few years ago—just completely froze up, couldn't breathe, had to go lay down in the guest bedroom for an hour. It was terrifying for her, I'm sure. \n\nBut for me, it's not like that. My heart rate might tick up a bit when I'm really dwelling on the practice buyout, or if I'm lying in bed at 3 a.m. running through the worst-case scenarios for my retirement portfolio, but it's not that sudden, overwhelming wave of terror you're describing. It's just... the worry is always there, kind of sitting on my shoulder, whispering in my ear. It's exhausting, but it doesn't peak and then go away. It just hums along, day in and day out, like a refrigerator motor you can't ignore. So, no, I haven't had those intense, out-of-the-blue episodes of panic or shortness of breath. Just the endless, grinding worry.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry spread across many different aspects of your life or is it mainly focused on one or two specific areas, like your retirement or the dental practice?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's... it's definitely not just one or two things, as much as I wish it were that simple. It really does spread out like a spiderweb across just about everything. I mean, yes, the retirement and the practice are the big, obvious ones right now. I mentioned how my mind keeps circling around all sorts of things with that—like, whether my old patients, people I've known for thirty years, are being looked after properly by the new associate. And then I worry about whether the practice handoff is financially sound, if we dotted every 'i' and crossed every 't' on the contracts. And, of course, the big existential dread of whether I’ll become completely useless once I’m fully retired and not going into the clinic every day. \n\nBut it doesn't stop there. It bleeds into everything else. I'll worry about my wife's health, even though she just had a physical and she's perfectly fine. I'll worry about my adult children—my daughter lives in Chicago, and if she doesn't text me back within a few hours, my brain immediately jumps to, 'Did she get into a car accident? Is she okay?' And then there's the house. We have an older home, built in the 70s, and every little creak in the floorboards makes me think the foundation is settling and we're going to need fifty thousand dollars in repairs. Or I'll watch the evening news and suddenly I'm agonizing over the state of the economy, or global politics, things I have absolutely zero control over. \n\nIt's just... it's exhausting because it latches onto whatever is in front of me. If I fix one problem, my brain just finds another one to chew on. And the worst part is, the worry feels completely out of proportion even to me. I know it's irrational to think my daughter is in a ditch just because she's in a meeting and can't check her phone, or to think the house is collapsing because of a squeaky floorboard. I know this logically. But the feeling, that heavy, churning anxiety, it just doesn't listen to logic. It just keeps spinning and spinning, jumping from the practice to the family to the house to the news, on and on. It's like I'm constantly bracing for an impact that never actually comes, but I can't stop bracing for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Restless... on edge... you mean like physically? Like pacing around the room or not being able to sit still? No, I don't think I'd say that. Actually, it's funny you ask, because physically, I'm almost the exact opposite. \n\nBeing a dentist for, gosh, nearly forty years now, you really have to train your body to be incredibly still. When you're doing a complex root canal or trying to place a crown perfectly, you have to hold your hands and your posture absolutely steady for long, long stretches of time. You're hunched over, hyper-focused, barely moving a muscle except your fingers. So my body is very used to just staying put, even when things are tense. \n\nEven at home, when my mind is racing a mile a minute about the practice buyout or whether my daughter in Chicago is safe, I'm usually just sitting completely still in my recliner. My wife will tell you—I can sit in that old leather chair in the den for three hours straight, staring at the evening news without really watching a single story, just completely lost in my own head, running through all those worst-case scenarios I mentioned. I don't pace the floors, I don't bounce my knee up and down, I don't fidget with my hands like some people do when they're nervous. It's really all trapped up here, in my brain. The mental gears are just grinding away, constantly, but the rest of my body is just... sitting there, perfectly quiet. So, no, I haven't really noticed feeling physically on edge or restless, like I need to jump out of my skin or move around. It's just my thoughts that refuse to sit still.\n\nDoctor: Has this constant worrying been interfering with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it has been getting in the way, more than I’d like to admit. It’s... it's frustrating, really. You see, with the practice, I’ve actually had to slow down the whole retirement handoff process. I was supposed to be transitioning more of the complex cases to the new associate by now, but I just keep second-guessing every single decision. I’ll look at a treatment plan we discussed, and my mind will just start spiraling—what if he misses a hidden canal? What if the margins on the crown aren't perfect and the patient comes back in pain in six months? So I end up stepping in, re-checking everything, taking over cases I should be handing off. It’s causing me to lose so much time at the clinic, and I know it’s probably frustrating the new associate, too, though he’s too polite to say anything. \n\nAnd it’s not just work, either. Even my hobbies... things I used to do to unwind. I’ve painted watercolors for twenty years, mostly landscapes, and I used to love spending a Sunday afternoon out in the garden. But lately, even those things don't feel as manageable as they used to. I’ll set up my easel, and instead of focusing on the canvas, I’m just sitting there, brush in hand, worrying about the clinic contracts or my daughter in Chicago. Or I’ll be out in the garden, trying to prune the roses, and I’m just consumed by this dread about the economy or the house foundation. I can't just be present and enjoy it anymore. The worry just kind of crowds everything else out, making even the simple, enjoyable things feel like a chore because my brain is just so preoccupied with all these... these endless scenarios. So, yes, it’s definitely taking a toll on how I function day-to-day.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Distress... yes, absolutely. It's profoundly distressing, doctor. I mean, it's not just a passing thought, like when you remember you forgot to take the trash out. It's this heavy, suffocating blanket that just settles over me. \n\nYou know, I've always prided myself on being a very capable person. As a dentist, people come to you in pain, and you fix it. You take control, you use your expertise, and you solve the problem. I've spent forty years doing that. But with this... this constant churning in my head, I can't fix it. I can't drill it out or put a crown on it. And that feeling of helplessness, of being a prisoner to my own runaway thoughts, is just incredibly upsetting. \n\nI'll be sitting there in my recliner at night, trying to watch a documentary with my wife—something about history, usually, something that should be relaxing—and instead of listening to the narrator, my chest feels tight, my stomach is in knots, and I'm just agonizing over the wording in section four of the transition contract, or wondering if my daughter remembered to get her snow tires put on before the blizzard hit Chicago. And I know it's ruining my evening, I know it's ruining my peace of mind, but I just can't turn the valve off. \n\nIt makes me feel so defeated. I've worked my whole life to get to this point, to be able to retire comfortably, to enjoy my family and my painting and my garden. This is supposed to be the golden time, isn't it? The reward for all those years of hard work. But instead, I'm just miserable. I'm exhausted from fighting my own brain from the moment I wake up until I finally manage to fall asleep. So, yes, it causes me a great deal of distress. It's stealing the joy right out of my life, and I just don't know how to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble meeting your responsibilities at work or at home due to your worrying?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose that goes right back to what I was just saying a few minutes ago about the practice. My main responsibility right now—my actual, primary job description at this stage in my career—is to manage this transition smoothly. But like I mentioned, I’ve had to officially slow down and postpone major parts of the retirement handoff. \n\nWe had a very strict timeline drawn up by the practice brokers, you see. I was supposed to have fully handed over the vendor accounts and the staff payroll management by the end of last month. But because I keep second-guessing every single decision, and obsessively re-checking the new associate's work like I told you, I'm losing enormous amounts of time at the clinic. I'll spend forty-five minutes agonizing over a file that should take me five minutes to approve. So I'm falling behind on the actual administrative responsibilities of the transition. The lawyers will send over the handoff documents, and instead of just reviewing and signing them, I stare at them for hours, paralyzed by all the \"what ifs.\" I'm just not meeting my obligations to the practice, to the new associate, or even to the brokers, because I'm just so bogged down in my own head. \n\nAnd at home... well, it’s the same story. I know I talked about my painting and my gardening earlier, and while you might just call those hobbies, keeping up the house and the yard is still a responsibility, you know? My wife relies on me for that. We have a large property, and it takes work. But lately, even those things just don't feel as manageable as they used to. The yard work is really piling up. The rose bushes need to be winterized, the raised beds need to be cleared out before the first hard freeze, and usually, I’d be right on top of it, out there every weekend until it was done perfectly. \n\nBut now? Now I just look out the window at the garden, and my brain is so exhausted from worrying about the clinic contracts and my daughter in Chicago and the state of the economy that I can't even bring myself to go out to the shed and get my tools. The canvases in my study are just sitting there blank, gathering dust, because I can't organize my thoughts enough to even mix the paints. So, yes, whether it's executing the clinic transition or just keeping up with my end of the bargain around the house, I'm definitely having trouble keeping up. The worry just eats up all my time and energy until there's absolutely nothing left for the things I'm actually supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Has your constant worrying affected your relationships with your family or friends?\n\nPatient: Relationships... well, you know, I’ve been thinking about that, and I wouldn't say it’s damaged anything, thank God. At least, not that I can see. \n\nMy wife, Eleanor, she’s an absolute saint. We’ll be married thirty-eight years this coming May, and she’s been with me through everything—dental school, taking out the loans to buy my first practice, raising the kids. So she knows me better than anyone. She knows I’ve always been a bit meticulous, a bit of a planner, though obviously this current situation is entirely different and much more severe. But she doesn't get angry with me. When I’m sitting there in the recliner, completely zoned out during one of our shows, she doesn't yell. She just sort of sighs, pauses the TV, and asks if I'm worrying about the transition contracts again. She’s incredibly patient. She'll make me a cup of chamomile tea and tell me to just put the paperwork out of my mind for the night. I know it must be exhausting for her to see me like this, and I feel terribly guilty about it, but we aren't fighting. I don't lash out at her, I just... I just retreat inward, I suppose. \n\nAnd as for my daughter in Chicago, Sarah... well, I probably call her a lot more often than a thirty-year-old woman wants to be called by her father. I’ll call her on a Tuesday afternoon just to make absolutely sure she remembered to get the oil changed in her Honda, or to remind her to double-lock her apartment door because I read some article about crime rates in her neighborhood. But she’s a good kid. She just laughs it off. She says, \"Yes, Dad, I did it, I'm fine.\" She doesn't get irritated, or if she does, she hides it well. \n\nEven with our friends... we have a regular dinner group, mostly a few other dentists from the local association and their wives. We went out just last Friday to this wonderful Italian place downtown, Luigi's, a place we've been going to for over a decade. And I have to admit, I wasn't exactly the life of the party. We were passing around the calamari, and I spent the entire appetizer course just staring at the tablecloth, agonizing over whether the new associate's malpractice insurance rider was filed correctly with the state board. But my friends, they just think I'm preoccupied with the logistics of retiring. They'll clap me on the shoulder and say, \"Hang in there, the golf course is calling!\" They don't realize the extent of the turmoil going on in my head. \n\nSo, no, I don't think it's driving people away or causing any real conflict. It's more of an internal torture, if that makes sense. I'm just incredibly lucky that the people around me are so understanding and give me so much grace, because I know I must be a bit of a drag to be around lately, constantly lost in my own head like this.\n\nDoctor: Is there any area of your life where you don't find yourself worrying?\n\nPatient: Any area where I don't worry? I... I wish I could give you a different answer, doctor, I really do. But honestly, no. There isn't a single quiet corner left in my head. It feels like my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things, constantly, without any sort of break. \n\nI'll be trying to eat breakfast, and suddenly I'm obsessing over whether my old patients are being looked after properly. I mean, I've treated some of these families for three generations. I know their dental histories, I know how nervous Mrs. Gable gets before a root canal, I know exactly how to adjust the chair for Mr. Henderson's bad back. Is the new associate taking the time to do all that, or is he just rushing them in and out like an assembly line? \n\nAnd then, before I can even convince myself they're fine, my brain jumps right over to the business side of things. I start agonizing over whether the practice handoff is financially sound. I've looked at the spreadsheets a hundred times, the brokers assure me the valuation is solid, the lawyers have reviewed it... but what if there's a loophole? What if the market dips, or the buyout structure falls apart, and everything I've built for the last forty years just crumbles financially at the eleventh hour? \n\nAnd it’s not just the clinic, either. It’s me. It’s this deep, creeping dread about what happens after the papers are actually signed. I keep wondering whether I’ll just become completely useless once I’m fully retired. I’ve been a dentist my entire adult life. People needed me to fix their pain. If I'm not doing that, if I don't have a schedule and a staff and patients relying on my expertise, who am I? Just some old man puttering around a garden that he can't even find the energy to weed right now? \n\nIt just bleeds into absolutely every corner of my life. And the most frustrating part—the part that really makes me feel like I'm losing my grip—is that I know, logically, that this worry feels out of proportion, even to me. I know the contracts are standard. I know the new associate graduated top of his class from a fantastic program. I know I have enough savings to retire comfortably. But my brain just refuses to accept the facts. It just keeps spinning and spinning, finding new things to latch onto, and I just can't seem to find a single area of my life that it hasn't infected.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to enjoy social activities or spend time with friends and family because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: Enjoy... well, I suppose \"enjoy\" is the operative word there, isn't it? Because I do still go to things. I haven't stopped showing up. Like I was just saying about that dinner at Luigi's with the other dentists, or... or even just sitting in the living room with Eleanor in the evenings. I don't find it difficult to actually be there, physically. I make a point of it, actually. \n\nWe have a Sunday brunch group, too, at the local club—we've been going for, gosh, it must be fifteen, maybe sixteen years now. We always try to get the same table right by the window overlooking the ninth hole. And the guys will be going on and on about their golf swings, or pulling out their phones to show pictures of their grandkids, and I'm there. I'm physically present. I'm eating my eggs benedict and drinking my coffee and paying the check. So I am definitely still spending time with them. I haven't locked myself in the study or completely withdrawn from society or anything dramatic like that. \n\nBut as for actually enjoying it? That’s... well, I'm not entirely sure. Because how can you truly enjoy a beautiful Sunday morning or a nice conversation when your brain is just buzzing, constantly buzzing, about transition timelines and lease agreements and whether the new associate is checking the margins on his crowns properly? I'm there, but I'm not really *there*, if that makes sense. I'm just sort of nodding and smiling, maybe throwing in a \"that's great, Bob\" here and there, while my mind is thirty miles away at the clinic, or worrying about Sarah's apartment security up in Chicago. \n\nSo, I wouldn't say I find it difficult to spend the time with people—my routine hasn't really changed in that regard—but the actual joy of it, the relaxation you're supposed to get from being around your friends and your wife... I haven't really noticed much of that lately. I'm not sure I'm capable of it right now. The worry just sort of acts like a heavy blanket over the whole event, muffling all the good parts until we can just go home and I can get back to my spreadsheets.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’ll look at a treatment plan we discussed, and my mind will just start spiraling—what if he misses a hidden canal? What if the margins on the crown aren't perfect and the patient comes back in pain in six months? So I end up stepping in, re-checking everything, taking over cases I should be handing off. It’s causing me to lose so much time at the clinic, and I know it’s probably frustrating the new associate, too, though he’s too polite to say anything. \n\nAnd it’s not just work, either. Even my hobbies... things I used to do to unwind. I’ve painted watercolors for twenty years, mostly landscapes, and I used to love spending a Sunday afternoon out in the garden. But lately, even those things don't feel as manageable as they used to. I’ll set up my easel, and instead of focusing on the canvas, I’m just sitting there, brush in hand, worrying about the clinic contracts or my daughter in Chicago. Or I’ll be out in the garden, trying to prune the roses, and I’m just consumed by this dread about the economy or the house foundation. I can't just be present and enjoy it anymore. The worry just kind of crowds everything else out, making even the simple, enjoyable things feel like a chore because my brain is just so preoccupied with all these... these endless scenarios. So, yes, it’s definitely taking a toll on how I function day-to-day.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Distress... yes, absolutely. It's profoundly distressing, doctor. I mean, it's not just a passing thought, like when you remember you forgot to take the trash out. It's this heavy, suffocating blanket that just settles over me. \n\nYou know, I've always prided myself on being a very capable person. As a dentist, people come to you in pain, and you fix it. You take control, you use your expertise, and you solve the problem. I've spent forty years doing that. But with this... this constant churning in my head, I can't fix it. I can't drill it out or put a crown on it. And that feeling of helplessness, of being a prisoner to my own runaway thoughts, is just incredibly upsetting. \n\nI'll be sitting there in my recliner at night, trying to watch a documentary with my wife—something about history, usually, something that should be relaxing—and instead of listening to the narrator, my chest feels tight, my stomach is in knots, and I'm just agonizing over the wording in section four of the transition contract, or wondering if my daughter remembered to get her snow tires put on before the blizzard hit Chicago. And I know it's ruining my evening, I know it's ruining my peace of mind, but I just can't turn the valve off. \n\nIt makes me feel so defeated. I've worked my whole life to get to this point, to be able to retire comfortably, to enjoy my family and my painting and my garden. This is supposed to be the golden time, isn't it? The reward for all those years of hard work. But instead, I'm just miserable. I'm exhausted from fighting my own brain from the moment I wake up until I finally manage to fall asleep. So, yes, it causes me a great deal of distress. It's stealing the joy right out of my life, and I just don't know how to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble meeting your responsibilities at work or at home due to your worrying?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose that goes right back to what I was just saying a few minutes ago about the practice. My main responsibility right now—my actual, primary job description at this stage in my career—is to manage this transition smoothly. But like I mentioned, I’ve had to officially slow down and postpone major parts of the retirement handoff. \n\nWe had a very strict timeline drawn up by the practice brokers, you see. I was supposed to have fully handed over the vendor accounts and the staff payroll management by the end of last month. But because I keep second-guessing every single decision, and obsessively re-checking the new associate's work like I told you, I'm losing enormous amounts of time at the clinic. I'll spend forty-five minutes agonizing over a file that should take me five minutes to approve. So I'm falling behind on the actual administrative responsibilities of the transition. The lawyers will send over the handoff documents, and instead of just reviewing and signing them, I stare at them for hours, paralyzed by all the \"what ifs.\" I'm just not meeting my obligations to the practice, to the new associate, or even to the brokers, because I'm just so bogged down in my own head. \n\nAnd at home... well, it’s the same story. I know I talked about my painting and my gardening earlier, and while you might just call those hobbies, keeping up the house and the yard is still a responsibility, you know? My wife relies on me for that. We have a large property, and it takes work. But lately, even those things just don't feel as manageable as they used to. The yard work is really piling up. The rose bushes need to be winterized, the raised beds need to be cleared out before the first hard freeze, and usually, I’d be right on top of it, out there every weekend until it was done perfectly. \n\nBut now? Now I just look out the window at the garden, and my brain is so exhausted from worrying about the clinic contracts and my daughter in Chicago and the state of the economy that I can't even bring myself to go out to the shed and get my tools. The canvases in my study are just sitting there blank, gathering dust, because I can't organize my thoughts enough to even mix the paints. So, yes, whether it's executing the clinic transition or just keeping up with my end of the bargain around the house, I'm definitely having trouble keeping up. The worry just eats up all my time and energy until there's absolutely nothing left for the things I'm actually supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Has your constant worrying affected your relationships with your family or friends?\n\nPatient: Relationships... well, you know, I’ve been thinking about that, and I wouldn't say it’s damaged anything, thank God. At least, not that I can see. \n\nMy wife, Eleanor, she’s an absolute saint. We’ll be married thirty-eight years this coming May, and she’s been with me through everything—dental school, taking out the loans to buy my first practice, raising the kids. So she knows me better than anyone. She knows I’ve always been a bit meticulous, a bit of a planner, though obviously this current situation is entirely different and much more severe. But she doesn't get angry with me. When I’m sitting there in the recliner, completely zoned out during one of our shows, she doesn't yell. She just sort of sighs, pauses the TV, and asks if I'm worrying about the transition contracts again. She’s incredibly patient. She'll make me a cup of chamomile tea and tell me to just put the paperwork out of my mind for the night. I know it must be exhausting for her to see me like this, and I feel terribly guilty about it, but we aren't fighting. I don't lash out at her, I just... I just retreat inward, I suppose. \n\nAnd as for my daughter in Chicago, Sarah... well, I probably call her a lot more often than a thirty-year-old woman wants to be called by her father. I’ll call her on a Tuesday afternoon just to make absolutely sure she remembered to get the oil changed in her Honda, or to remind her to double-lock her apartment door because I read some article about crime rates in her neighborhood. But she’s a good kid. She just laughs it off. She says, \"Yes, Dad, I did it, I'm fine.\" She doesn't get irritated, or if she does, she hides it well. \n\nEven with our friends... we have a regular dinner group, mostly a few other dentists from the local association and their wives. We went out just last Friday to this wonderful Italian place downtown, Luigi's, a place we've been going to for over a decade. And I have to admit, I wasn't exactly the life of the party. We were passing around the calamari, and I spent the entire appetizer course just staring at the tablecloth, agonizing over whether the new associate's malpractice insurance rider was filed correctly with the state board. But my friends, they just think I'm preoccupied with the logistics of retiring. They'll clap me on the shoulder and say, \"Hang in there, the golf course is calling!\" They don't realize the extent of the turmoil going on in my head. \n\nSo, no, I don't think it's driving people away or causing any real conflict. It's more of an internal torture, if that makes sense. I'm just incredibly lucky that the people around me are so understanding and give me so much grace, because I know I must be a bit of a drag to be around lately, constantly lost in my own head like this.\n\nDoctor: Is there any area of your life where you don't find yourself worrying?\n\nPatient: Any area where I don't worry? I... I wish I could give you a different answer, doctor, I really do. But honestly, no. There isn't a single quiet corner left in my head. It feels like my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things, constantly, without any sort of break. \n\nI'll be trying to eat breakfast, and suddenly I'm obsessing over whether my old patients are being looked after properly. I mean, I've treated some of these families for three generations. I know their dental histories, I know how nervous Mrs. Gable gets before a root canal, I know exactly how to adjust the chair for Mr. Henderson's bad back. Is the new associate taking the time to do all that, or is he just rushing them in and out like an assembly line? \n\nAnd then, before I can even convince myself they're fine, my brain jumps right over to the business side of things. I start agonizing over whether the practice handoff is financially sound. I've looked at the spreadsheets a hundred times, the brokers assure me the valuation is solid, the lawyers have reviewed it... but what if there's a loophole? What if the market dips, or the buyout structure falls apart, and everything I've built for the last forty years just crumbles financially at the eleventh hour? \n\nAnd it’s not just the clinic, either. It’s me. It’s this deep, creeping dread about what happens after the papers are actually signed. I keep wondering whether I’ll just become completely useless once I’m fully retired. I’ve been a dentist my entire adult life. People needed me to fix their pain. If I'm not doing that, if I don't have a schedule and a staff and patients relying on my expertise, who am I? Just some old man puttering around a garden that he can't even find the energy to weed right now? \n\nIt just bleeds into absolutely every corner of my life. And the most frustrating part—the part that really makes me feel like I'm losing my grip—is that I know, logically, that this worry feels out of proportion, even to me. I know the contracts are standard. I know the new associate graduated top of his class from a fantastic program. I know I have enough savings to retire comfortably. But my brain just refuses to accept the facts. It just keeps spinning and spinning, finding new things to latch onto, and I just can't seem to find a single area of my life that it hasn't infected.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to enjoy social activities or spend time with friends and family because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: Enjoy... well, I suppose \"enjoy\" is the operative word there, isn't it? Because I do still go to things. I haven't stopped showing up. Like I was just saying about that dinner at Luigi's with the other dentists, or... or even just sitting in the living room with Eleanor in the evenings. I don't find it difficult to actually be there, physically. I make a point of it, actually. \n\nWe have a Sunday brunch group, too, at the local club—we've been going for, gosh, it must be fifteen, maybe sixteen years now. We always try to get the same table right by the window overlooking the ninth hole. And the guys will be going on and on about their golf swings, or pulling out their phones to show pictures of their grandkids, and I'm there. I'm physically present. I'm eating my eggs benedict and drinking my coffee and paying the check. So I am definitely still spending time with them. I haven't locked myself in the study or completely withdrawn from society or anything dramatic like that. \n\nBut as for actually enjoying it? That’s... well, I'm not entirely sure. Because how can you truly enjoy a beautiful Sunday morning or a nice conversation when your brain is just buzzing, constantly buzzing, about transition timelines and lease agreements and whether the new associate is checking the margins on his crowns properly? I'm there, but I'm not really *there*, if that makes sense. I'm just sort of nodding and smiling, maybe throwing in a \"that's great, Bob\" here and there, while my mind is thirty miles away at the clinic, or worrying about Sarah's apartment security up in Chicago. \n\nSo, I wouldn't say I find it difficult to spend the time with people—my routine hasn't really changed in that regard—but the actual joy of it, the relaxation you're supposed to get from being around your friends and your wife... I haven't really noticed much of that lately. I'm not sure I'm capable of it right now. The worry just sort of acts like a heavy blanket over the whole event, muffling all the good parts until we can just go home and I can get back to my spreadsheets.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Interfering... well, yes, I suppose it is. I mean, if I’m being completely honest about how my days are actually going, it’s... it’s definitely taking a toll. 

At the clinic, for example, I was supposed to have the transition timeline completely finalized by the end of last month. The broker had everything laid out, a very neat, orderly schedule for handing over the keys, so to speak. But I’ve had to slow the whole thing down. I’ve actually had to call the lawyers and postpone parts of the retirement handoff because I just... I keep second-guessing every single decision. I’ll sit in my office—my private office in the back, the one with the good window overlooking Elm Street—and I'll have the final documents right in front of me. But instead of reviewing them and signing, I just lose time. Hours of time, just agonizing over whether the equipment valuation is fair, or if the new associate is really prepared to take over the complex restorative cases. I'll just sit there, completely paralyzed, losing all this valuable time at the clinic when I should be wrapping things up or saying goodbye to my staff. 

And then, you know, you asked about daily activities, and it’s bleeding into those, too. I’ve always been a very active person outside of work. I paint—watercolors, mostly landscapes, just a little amateur hobby I picked up a decade ago—and I've always loved gardening. Eleanor and I have these beautiful hybrid tea roses out back that require a lot of very specific, careful pruning. But lately... none of it feels manageable anymore. I look out the window at the garden, and I know the roses need tending, I know the weeds are starting to come up near the hydrangeas, but the thought of going out there and actually doing the work just feels overwhelming. 

The same goes for my painting. I’ll set up the easel in the sunroom, get my palette out, pour the water, and then I just stare at the blank paper. I can't focus on the colors or the brushstrokes because my mind is a million miles away, worrying about the practice and the transition and everything else. It’s like I just don't have the mental bandwidth or the energy left for the things that used to bring me peace. So, yes, it really is interfering, much more than I’d like to admit. I just can't seem to function the way I used to, neither at the office nor at home.

I know the contracts are standard. I know the new associate graduated top of his class from a fantastic program. I know I have enough savings to retire comfortably. But my brain just refuses to accept the facts. It just keeps spinning and spinning, finding new things to latch onto, and I just can't seem to find a single area of my life that it hasn't infected.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to enjoy social activities or spend time with friends and family because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: Enjoy... well, I suppose \"enjoy\" is the operative word there, isn't it? I start thinking about the clinic's lease renewal, or if my grandson is doing okay in his new school, or if that slight rattle in the car engine means the transmission is failing... and suddenly I'm not painting, I'm not reading the chart, I'm just sitting there paralyzed by all these thoughts. But my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things, constantly. If I'm not doing that, who am I? It just goes on and on like that. And the frustrating part is, I know it's irrational. The worry feels entirely out of proportion, even to me. I can look at the financials and see they're fine, but the feeling of impending doom just doesn't care about the spreadsheets. It's just... always there, humming away, making me second-guess absolutely everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes and includes symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I've ever had anything quite like that. It's not... it's not a sudden explosion, if you know what I mean. It's more of a slow burn, this constant, simmering tension that just never seems to boil over but also never cools down. I don't get those moments where my heart is pounding out of my chest or I'm gasping for air, like you see in the movies when someone is having a panic attack. It was terrifying for her, I'm sure. \n\nBut for me, it's not like that. My heart rate might tick up a bit when I'm really dwelling on the practice buyout, or if I'm lying in bed at 3 a.m. running through the worst-case scenarios for my retirement portfolio, but it's not that sudden, overwhelming wave of terror you're describing. It's just... the worry is always there, kind of sitting on my shoulder, whispering in my ear. It's exhausting, but it doesn't peak and then go away. It just hums along, day in and day out, like a refrigerator motor you can't ignore. So, no, I haven't had those intense, out-of-the-blue episodes of panic or shortness of breath. Just the endless, grinding worry.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry spread across many different aspects of your life or is it mainly focused on one or two specific areas, like your retirement or the dental practice?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's... it's definitely not just one or two things, as much as I wish it were that simple. It really does spread out like a spiderweb across just about everything. I mean, yes, the retirement and the practice are the big, obvious ones right now. I mentioned how my mind keeps circling around all sorts of things with that—like, whether my old patients, people I've known for thirty years, are being looked after properly by the new associate. I know it's irrational to think my daughter is in a ditch just because she's in a meeting and can't check her phone, or to think the house is collapsing because of a squeaky floorboard. I know this logically. But the feeling, that heavy, churning anxiety, it just doesn't listen to logic. It just keeps spinning and spinning, jumping from the practice to the family to the house to the news, on and on. Actually, it's funny you ask, because physically, I'm almost the exact opposite. \n\nBeing a dentist for, gosh, nearly forty years now, you really have to train your body to be incredibly still. When you're doing a complex root canal or trying to place a crown perfectly, you have to hold your hands and your posture absolutely steady for long, long stretches of time. You're hunched over, hyper-focused, barely moving a muscle except your fingers. So my body is very used to just staying put, even when things are tense. \n\nEven at home, when my mind is racing a mile a minute about the practice buyout or whether my daughter in Chicago is safe, I'm usually just sitting completely still in my recliner. My wife will tell you—I can sit in that old leather chair in the den for three hours straight, staring at the evening news without really watching a single story, just completely lost in my own head, running through all those worst-case scenarios I mentioned. I don't pace the floors, I don't bounce my knee up and down, I don't fidget with my hands like some people do when they're nervous. It's really all trapped up here, in my brain. The mental gears are just grinding away, constantly, but the rest of my body is just... sitting there, perfectly quiet. So, no, I haven't really noticed feeling physically on edge or restless, like I need to jump out of my skin or move around. It's just my thoughts that refuse to sit still.\n\nDoctor: Has this constant worrying been interfering with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it has been getting in the way, more than I’d like to admit. It’s... it's frustrating, really. You see, with the practice, I’ve actually had to slow down the whole retirement handoff process. I was supposed to be transitioning more of the complex cases to the new associate by now, but I just keep second-guessing every single decision. I’ll look at a treatment plan we discussed, and my mind will just start spiraling—what if he misses a hidden canal? What if the margins on the crown aren't perfect and the patient comes back in pain in six months? So I end up stepping in, re-checking everything, taking over cases I should be handing off. It’s causing me to lose so much time at the clinic, and I know it’s probably frustrating the new associate, too, though he’s too polite to say anything. \n\nAnd it’s not just work, either. Even my hobbies... things I used to do to unwind. I’ve painted watercolors for twenty years, mostly landscapes, and I used to love spending a Sunday afternoon out in the garden. But lately, even those things don't feel as manageable as they used to. I’ll set up my easel, and instead of focusing on the canvas, I’m just sitting there, brush in hand, worrying about the clinic contracts or my daughter in Chicago. Or I’ll be out in the garden, trying to prune the roses, and I’m just consumed by this dread about the economy or the house foundation. I can't just be present and enjoy it anymore. The worry just kind of crowds everything else out, making even the simple, enjoyable things feel like a chore because my brain is just so preoccupied with all these... these endless scenarios. So, yes, it’s definitely taking a toll on how I function day-to-day.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Distress... yes, absolutely. It's profoundly distressing, doctor. I mean, it's not just a passing thought, like when you remember you forgot to take the trash out. It's this heavy, suffocating blanket that just settles over me. \n\nYou know, I've always prided myself on being a very capable person. As a dentist, people come to you in pain, and you fix it. You take control, you use your expertise, and you solve the problem. I've spent forty years doing that. But with this... this constant churning in my head, I can't fix it. I can't drill it out or put a crown on it. And that feeling of helplessness, of being a prisoner to my own runaway thoughts, is just incredibly upsetting. \n\nI'll be sitting there in my recliner at night, trying to watch a documentary with my wife—something about history, usually, something that should be relaxing—and instead of listening to the narrator, my chest feels tight, my stomach is in knots, and I'm just agonizing over the wording in section four of the transition contract, or wondering if my daughter remembered to get her snow tires put on before the blizzard hit Chicago. And I know it's ruining my evening, I know it's ruining my peace of mind, but I just can't turn the valve off. \n\nIt makes me feel so defeated. I've worked my whole life to get to this point, to be able to retire comfortably, to enjoy my family and my painting and my garden. This is supposed to be the golden time, isn't it? The reward for all those years of hard work. But instead, I'm just miserable. I'm exhausted from fighting my own brain from the moment I wake up until I finally manage to fall asleep. So, yes, it causes me a great deal of distress. It's stealing the joy right out of my life, and I just don't know how to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble meeting your responsibilities at work or at home due to your worrying?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose that goes right back to what I was just saying a few minutes ago about the practice.
